\title{\vspace{-2em}The Labyrinth}
\author{\normalsize A generated dungeon, decomposed into container combinators}
\date{}

\begin{document}
\maketitle
\vspace{-2em}

\section{Description of the game}
\label{sec:description}

\begin{sectionbox}
A party of three descends into a labyrinth, armed with daggers, to receive a sacred item and comes
back up. The labyrinth is a tree of depth eight. It is generated as they descend and it does not
exist until somebody tries to go there. The code of each level is generated by Claude, from a
specification of that level which is also generated by Claude. The game carries an element of
chance and the difficulty level can be configured.

\medskip

Each level contains a maze (generated by Claude when he generates the level). Some of the rooms contain monsters and some of them don't. Some
of the doors are guarded by monsters. You can either kill the monster and take its items, try to
convince the monster to open the door, or trade your items with the monster. Trap doors lead down
into lower levels of the labyrinth. These are guarded by gate-keepers. To convince the gate-keeper
to open the trap door you must solve a riddle, so some of the monsters also have clues.

\medskip

There are five types of monster and each type of monster has secrets and desires and subjects they
enjoy talking about. The monsters are LLMs. You can either negotiate with them, chat with them,
trade with them or try to kill them.

\medskip

The monsters also have relics. There are ten types of relic, each of a different weight. The party
can only carry 6 at a time (6 hands) one for each hand - including a dagger and a lamp. You can
collect healing potions and lamp oil but these must be carried.

\medskip

You must discover the monsters' secrets and desires to solve the labyrinth. It is a turn-based console game with a finite
list of possible moves at each turn - including negotiating with and chatting to the monsters. The monsters can lie, and some of the other monsters know that
they are lying.

\medskip

You can also come back up the labyrinth. The most valuable relics lie deepest in the labyrinth and
you must go down into the labyrinth to collect them then come back up with the items to negotiate
with the monsters who guard the doors on higher levels. The sacred object is on the top level
guarded by a monster that you must trade with to open the door using a relic from the lowest level.

\medskip

In the future we could add a simple GUI with a drop down menu of options, a
way to view your inventory and an image for each room, monster and item (generated by Claude from a
description using an image generator). Claude also generates the descriptions.

\medskip

The point of the game is to illustrate agentic container programming. That Claude can also generate
the specification, monster prompts, and images from descriptions is an added twist. Claude ``optimizes'' the parameters to make sure it is
actually solvable and ``interesting''. Whether or not the game really is interesting to play
remains an open question.

\medskip

Claude draws from history, religion and mythology to add an element of creativity to the game. It
could also be sci-fi.
\end{sectionbox}

\section{The game}
\label{sec:game}

\subsection{The sacred item is given, not taken}
\label{sec:sacred}

\begin{center}
\begin{tcolorbox}[plainbg, colback=pale, width=0.9\textwidth]
\begin{align*}
\text{run} \;&=\; \text{descend}^{8} \;\lhd\; \text{receive} \;\lhd\; \text{ascend}^{8} \\
\text{receive} \;&=\; \text{ask} \;\seqb\; \bigl( \text{given} \;+\; \text{refused} \bigr)
\end{align*}
\end{tcolorbox}
\end{center}

Somewhere in the
labyrinth is somebody holding the sacred item, and it will hand it over if it is satisfied. It cannot
be killed for it, cannot be robbed of it, and there is no other copy.

\begin{sectionbox}
Which makes the table of Section~\ref{sec:table} the \emph{price} rather than the prize. Nobody wins
by knowing what things want. They win by being given a thing, and knowing what things want is what
it costs. That is a better shape for an objective, because a player understands \emph{fetch the
sacred item} immediately and would never have understood \emph{learn the table}.
\end{sectionbox}

It also generalises Section~\ref{sec:living} to the goal itself. That section makes some ways
\emph{operated} rather than merely guarded, meaning the thing standing there is the only thing that
can open them and its corpse cannot, so that violence is not a complete strategy. The sacred item is
the same rule applied once more, at the one place where it settles the whole game.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{killing works} & \textbf{satisfying works} \\
\midrule
a guarded doorway   & yes & yes \\
an operated doorway & no  & yes \\
\textbf{the sacred item} & \textbf{no, and there is no second attempt} & \textbf{yes} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

And the first line of the algebra says the run is symmetric and it is not. Descending eight levels
costs light and nerve. Ascending eight costs light and \emph{table}, because
Section~\ref{sec:table} says the ways home are operated and each wants something particular. So the
exponent on the right is the expensive one, which is the oldest claim in this note and now it is the
shape of the run.

\subsection{Where it stands is a parameter}
\label{sec:goal}

Nothing above says which level the gate-keeper of the sacred item is on, and that is deliberate, because
the two ends of the choice are two different games.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.56\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{at the deepest level} & the descent is the whole quest and the ascent is getting out alive
with what you were given \\[1mm]
\textbf{near the surface}     & the descent is shopping, and the last act is a trade at the top paid
for with something carried up from the bottom \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Two knobs settle it. \texttt{goalDepth} is the level its gate-keeper stands on, and \texttt{goalReach} is
how far below that gate-keeper the thing which satisfies it is found.

\begin{sectionbox}
Which is Section~\ref{sec:puzzlepieces} applied to the goal rather than to a doorway. That section
says the labyrinth must be unlocked from below, and a \texttt{goalReach} of nought is the one
setting where that is not true of the goal itself. So this is not a detail of placement. It decides
whether the ascent is where the game ends or merely where it survives, which is the claim
Section~\ref{sec:updown} has been making about every other doorway.
\end{sectionbox}

\begin{alarmbox}
And one thing here is a condition and never a rate. \textbf{Whatever the two are set to, the thing
that satisfies the sacred item must be findable, and carriable to its gate-keeper, inside the light, the
hands and the descents.} A \texttt{goalReach} the party cannot physically service is a labyrinth
that cannot be finished for a reason no player could see, which Section~\ref{sec:prob2} says is
never left to a draw. It joins the conditions checked when the level is written and repaired if
violated.
\end{alarmbox}

\subsection{What it costs the tuning}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.40\textwidth}@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{}}
\toprule
\texttt{goalDepth}  & level the sacred item's gate-keeper stands on & 1 to \texttt{depth} \\
\texttt{goalReach}  & levels below that gate-keeper its price is found & 0 to \texttt{depth}$-1$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\textbf{Where the run ended.} The criterion, and it is the one that says whether the knob did
anything. Of the runs that got the sacred item, what fraction spent their last descent climbing
rather than digging. All digging and \texttt{goalReach} is decoration. All climbing and the depth of
the labyrinth is scenery the player passes through once.

\subsection{A tree of depth eight, and where the cycles sit in it}

One more reconciliation, because Section~\ref{sec:defn} describes two recursions and this describes
one tree, and they are the same object.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.56\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{the tree}    & the labyrinth. Rooms are its nodes, doorways are its edges, and its depth is
eight \\[1mm]
\textbf{a level}     & the set of nodes at one depth. Not a thing in its own right, which is why the
algebra of Section~\ref{sec:defn} has no line for it \\[1mm]
\textbf{the chords}  & the extra doorways of Section~\ref{sec:cycles}. A tree with chords is not a
tree, and they are added to it rather than being part of it \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{sectionbox}
So the tree is the skeleton and the cycles are chords across it. Which is exactly why
Section~\ref{sec:cycles} could allow them for nothing. The tree is what generation builds, and it
builds downward, so the depth function stays well defined. A chord is a doorway pointed at a room
that already exists, and pointing at something costs a well-founded structure nothing at all.
\end{sectionbox}

Depth eight is a parameter and eight is a guess. It is \texttt{depth}, it lives in the clocks block
of Section~\ref{sec:allknobs}, and Section~\ref{sec:ratio} is how its real value gets found. Too
shallow and the table is learned before it is needed. Too deep and the ascent costs more knowledge
than a party could have gathered on the way down, which is $\rho > 1$ and is the one failure that
makes a labyrinth unplayable rather than merely hard.

\subsection{What is inside a room, what a level is, and the two recursions}
\label{sec:defn}

\begin{center}
\begin{tcolorbox}[plainbg, colback=pale, width=0.9\textwidth]
\begin{align*}
\text{labyrinth} \;&=\; \text{room}(1) \\
\text{room}(d)   \;&=\; \text{round} \;\lhd\; \text{room}(d) \;\lhd\; \text{exit}(d) \\
\text{exit}(d)   \;&=\; e_1 \;\times\; e_2 \;\times\; \cdots \;\times\; e_k \\
e_i              \;&=\; \text{room}(d) \;+\; \text{room}(d{+}1) \\
\text{room}(9)   \;&=\; \text{nothing. There is no ninth level}
\end{align*}
\end{tcolorbox}
\end{center}

Read the fourth line, because it answers a question the rest of this note assumed without ever
saying. A doorway leads either to another room \emph{at the same depth} or to a room one level down.
So yes, you descend recursively into rooms on the same level, and a level is a tree rather than a
row.

\subsection{What is inside a room}

Five things and no more, which is what makes a generated one checkable.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.34\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{how many} & \textbf{and} \\
\midrule
\textbf{squares}    & $w \times h$ & a rectangle. Section~\ref{sec:grid} is about what the
squares are for, which is distance and nothing else \\[1mm]
\textbf{an inhabitant} & nought or one & standing on a square. A guard, a roamer or a civilian, per
Section~\ref{sec:inhabitants} \\[1mm]
\textbf{an item}    & nought or one & lying on a square, and Section~\ref{sec:items} says what
kinds there are \\[1mm]
\textbf{doorways}   & one or more   & on the boundary. Each one leads laterally or downward, and
which is which is the level's business rather than yours \\[1mm]
\textbf{the way you came in} & one & which is a doorway, taken backwards \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{sectionbox}
Note what a room does \emph{not} contain. No party of monsters, because
Section~\ref{sec:oneguard} reduced it to one. No hoard as a separate thing, because a hoard was only
ever an item lying in a room. And nothing behind a locked way, because the locked way is a doorway
and the inhabitant standing next to it is the lock.
\end{sectionbox}

A room with no inhabitant is where you can rest, which is Section~\ref{sec:rest}. A room with no
item and no inhabitant is corridor, and there should be some.

\subsection{So a level is a tree and there are two recursions}

A level is every room reachable at one depth, and it is a tree because doorways branch. Which means
this note now has two recursions where it had one, and they need separate bounds.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.30\textwidth}@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.28\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{recurses} & \textbf{bounded by} \\
\midrule
\textbf{deeper}  & $\text{room}(d) \to \text{room}(d{+}1)$, which is the descent &
\texttt{depth}, which is why Section~\ref{sec:recursion} terminates \\[1mm]
\textbf{further} & $\text{room}(d) \to \text{room}(d)$, which is walking about &
\texttt{roomsPerLevel}, and it must be a \emph{total} rather than a fan-out \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{alarmbox}
The second bound has to be a total or the generator writes an unbounded level. Three doorways out of
every room with no cap on the count is a labyrinth in which no trap door is ever reached, and a party
inside one runs out of light having descended nothing. So a level is at most $n$ rooms, the
generator counts them, and at least one of them holds a doorway going down.
\end{alarmbox}

\subsection{Rooms may cycle. Levels may not}
\label{sec:cycles}

\begin{center}
\begin{tcolorbox}[plainbg, colback=pale, width=0.9\textwidth]
\begin{align*}
\textbf{creation} \;&:\; \text{a new room is at depth } d \text{ or } d{+}1, \text{ never shallower} \\
\textbf{reference} \;&:\; \text{a doorway may point at any room that already exists}
\end{align*}
\end{tcolorbox}
\end{center}

Those two lines are the whole answer and the distinction between them is what makes it safe.
Generation only ever goes down or sideways. Pointing goes anywhere.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.34\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{allowed} & \textbf{and} \\
\midrule
a cycle among rooms at one depth & \textbf{yes} & walk through three doorways and come back to
where you started \\[1mm]
a doorway pointing up to a level you have seen & \textbf{yes} & it is a shortcut home, and it is a
reference rather than a creation \\[1mm]
a cycle among \emph{levels} & \textbf{no} & nothing at depth $d$ may lead to a \emph{new} room
shallower than $d$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{Why cycles cost nothing here}

Because Section~\ref{sec:defn} made the lateral bound a total rather than a fan-out.

A recursion bounded by path length would be broken by a cycle, since a cycle has no longest path. A
recursion bounded by the number of rooms is not, because going round a loop revisits rooms rather
than creating them, and the count does not move. So the termination argument survives untouched and
the thing that saved it was a decision taken for an unrelated reason.

\begin{sectionbox}
Which is the same shape as Section~\ref{sec:promise}. There, promises are created downward and
discharged wherever. Here, rooms are created downward and referenced wherever. In both cases what
keeps the whole thing well-founded is that \emph{creation} is monotone in depth and nothing else has
to be.
\end{sectionbox}

One cost, and it is a real change to the generator. Lazily writing a fresh room behind every doorway
is no longer enough, because a cycle means two doorways lead to the same room. So the generator
must be able to point a doorway at a room that exists, which means it has to know which rooms exist,
which is level state and Section~\ref{sec:roles} says a level holds state anyway.

\subsection{What a cycle buys}

Three things, and the third is the one worth having.

\textbf{The map acquires topology.} A tree map is a path with branches you did not take. A cyclic
map has shape, and remembering it is worth something, which is what Section~\ref{sec:map} claims and
could not previously have justified.

\textbf{A cycle is knowledge, and it is earned.} You find it by walking into a room you recognise.
Nothing tells you, nobody sells it, and no rule predicts it. It is the one row of the table that is
discovered purely by having been somewhere twice.

\textbf{And the way back stops being the way you came.} This is the sharp one. In a tree, retreating
costs exactly what advancing cost, so the arithmetic of the return is known the moment you arrive.
In a loop it is not, and a party far into one may find that going \emph{on} is cheaper than going
back.

\begin{alarmbox}
Which is push-your-luck arithmetic done with geometry instead of probability. Section~\ref{sec:light}
says half the lamp is the way home, and that reservation assumed the way home was the way in. In a
cyclic level it might be less, and it might be more, and finding out costs the rounds you were
saving. A party that guesses right has bought depth with topology. A party that guesses wrong is in
the dark in a room it has already seen.
\end{alarmbox}

\subsection{And the shortcut home is the oldest theme in the note}

The second row of the first table deserves its own paragraph.

Section~\ref{sec:puzzlepieces} says the labyrinth must be unlocked from below and that nothing found
at the bottom is worth anything until it has been carried up. A doorway from depth six to depth two,
openable only from the six side, is that sentence made into a piece of architecture.

\begin{sectionbox}
And it is a reference and not a creation, so it costs the termination argument nothing. The level it
points at was written long ago. What the party opened was not a new part of the labyrinth. It was a
second way into a part they already knew, which is what a shortcut is, and it is the only thing in
the game that makes a later descent cheaper than an earlier one.
\end{sectionbox}

\subsection{Which room you are in is not in the algebra}
\label{sec:whichroom}

\begin{center}
\begin{tcolorbox}[plainbg, colback=pale, width=0.9\textwidth]
\begin{align*}
\text{the shape} \;&:\; \texttt{\{tag:"Move", to: Square\}} \quad\text{or a doorway} \\
\text{the position} \;&:\; \texttt{\{ w, h, inhabitant?, item?, doorways \}}
\end{align*}
\end{tcolorbox}
\end{center}

Read the second line and notice what is absent from it. There is no name, no number and no
identifier. The response \textbf{describes} the room you are now in and it does not
\textbf{identify} it.

That is deliberate, and it follows from what a container is rather than from a design preference. A
container is a set of prompts and a response type for each. Which container you are addressing is
not part of the type, it is which runner you sent to, and in the tower that is a port. So room
identity was never going to be in the algebra, because identity of the thing being prompted is
always outside it.

\begin{sectionbox}
Which is Section~\ref{sec:crosses} arriving somewhere concrete. The boundary carries a description
and nothing else. A level that answered \emph{you are in room seventeen} would be naming its own
internal structure across a boundary, and the note has spent forty pages arguing that nothing may
do that.
\end{sectionbox}

\subsection{So recognising a room is inference, and it can be wrong}

Which turns the question into a mechanic rather than a gap.

You know where you are by what is there. Ten squares by four, a doorway on each of three sides,
nobody in it, an empty lamp oil flask on the floor. That is a description and you have seen descriptions
before, so you compare.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.56\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{it works} & when rooms differ. A room with a civilian in it who told you something is
unmistakable \\[1mm]
\textbf{it fails} & when they do not. Two empty rooms of the same dimensions with three doorways
are the same room as far as anybody can tell \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{alarmbox}
And Section~\ref{sec:cycles} makes that failure expensive. A cycle you did not notice is a cycle you
walk round twice, and the second circuit costs exactly as much light as the first while
accomplishing nothing whatever. The generator may make two rooms alike on purpose, so
\texttt{confusableRate} is a knob, and it is the only knob in the note that spends the player's
light without their ever finding out why.
\end{alarmbox}

Which also demotes the map of Section~\ref{sec:map} from a record to a \emph{belief}. It is the
client's own reconstruction from descriptions, nothing validates it, and it can be wrong in the
particular way that two identical rooms make it wrong. So the map joins the table as a thing the
player builds and may build incorrectly.

\subsection{Chalk, which is identity for one turn}

The cheapest mechanic in the note and it makes the whole problem an economic one.

You can mark a room. It costs a turn, it costs nothing else, and thereafter that room answers with a
description that includes your mark.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.56\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{what it buys} & certainty about identity, permanently, for one round of light \\[1mm]
\textbf{what it costs} & one round, which is one round of conversation not had, or one square not
walked \\[1mm]
\textbf{where it lives} & in the room's state, not in the party's record, so
Section~\ref{sec:roles} puts it with the corpses and the opened ways \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{sectionbox}
So the answer to \emph{how do you know which room you are in} is the answer to every other question
in this game. You do not know, you can find out, and finding out costs light. Which is the property
Section~\ref{sec:fits} says the whole design has, arriving in the one place nobody would have
thought to look for it.
\end{sectionbox}

And the mark is state rather than an index, so Section~\ref{sec:freedom} says the generator may let
you write anything you like on a wall. What a party wrote on the walls of level four two descents ago
is a message from itself, and nothing in the labyrinth reads it but them.

\subsection{And that is what makes light a geometry rather than a depth}

Worth drawing out, because the lateral recursion is load-bearing and not a flourish.

If a level were three rooms in a row then the way home from anywhere would be one step, and light
would price nothing but depth. With a tree the way home is the path back through the rooms you came
through, \emph{and then} the levels above.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.56\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\S\ref{sec:rest}      & rest needs an empty room, so now there is somewhere to walk to and a price
for having gone the other way \\[1mm]
\S\ref{sec:oneguard}  & retreat is how you decline a price, so now there is somewhere to retreat to
\\[1mm]
\S\ref{sec:map}       & the map is worth keeping, because a tree is a thing you can be lost in and a
row of three is not \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

So \emph{as far as you dare} has two meanings at once and they compete for one lamp. How deep, and
how far in on this level, with half the lamp reserved for undoing both.

\begin{sectionbox}
Which is the last thing the arithmetic needed. Section~\ref{sec:table} makes depth a debt
denominated in knowledge. Lateral distance is a debt denominated in light, and unlike the first one
it falls due on this trip.
\end{sectionbox}

\subsection{What the game actually is}
\label{sec:thesis}

One sentence, and the rest of this note is the machinery under it.

\begin{sectionbox}
\textbf{You have to figure out what the inhabitants want. That is the game, and
Section~\ref{sec:table} says what the thing you are figuring out actually is.}
\end{sectionbox}

Everything else is a price on that search. The note arrives at a good many mechanisms and it is
worth saying up front that not one of them is a separate subsystem. Each one makes finding out what
something wants cost more, or make less sense, or matter later.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{5mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.58\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
light            & prices the asking. Every question is a round \\
hands            & price the paying. What you can carry bounds what you can offer \\
the desire graph & makes the wants refer to each other, so finding one is finding a path \\
its settling      & means the wants are answered by each other while you are away, so knowing late
is knowing nothing \\
the roamers       & carry secrets about and trade in the open, so what they take can be watched and
cannot be a lie \\
motives to lie   & make the answers unreliable in a structured way, so you reason rather than trust \\
the colour rule  & means the wants have a pattern, drawn fresh each game, so you generalise as well
as deduce \\
the interests    & mean a thing may simply tell you, if you talk about what it likes, and that is the
only free thing here \\
leakage          & makes a secret sellable once, so knowing is not the same as profiting \\
descents         & make the search finite, so an unanswered question stays unanswered \\
recruits         & are wants that persist, so a satisfied monster keeps costing \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Read the list as one claim. The labyrinth is a set of wants, most of them hidden, some of them
about each other, held by things that may misdescribe their own. A player's whole activity is
finding out what they are and deciding which to satisfy, and every resource in the game exists to
make that activity cost something.

\begin{sectionbox}
Which is also why the monsters had to be language models and could not have been a menu. A want you
discover by selecting \emph{ask about desire} from a list is a want the game told you. A want you
discover by talking, from something that may deflect, misdirect or name a price for saying, is one
you found out. The non-determinism is not decoration on the search. It is what makes it a search.
\end{sectionbox}

\subsection{The table, and why knowing it is the way out}
\label{sec:table}

Before a game begins, Claude draws up a table. Nobody sees it. It is different every time.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.38\textwidth}@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{axis} & \textbf{what is drawn} & \textbf{rows} \\
\midrule
kinds of inhabitant & the flavours that exist, and what each kind wants & a handful \\[1mm]
colours of item     & which colour of thing each kind of inhabitant wants & a mapping \\[1mm]
topics of interest  & what each individual likes talking about & one apiece \\[1mm]
motives             & who lies, and about what & one apiece \\[1mm]
secrets             & who knows which true thing & one apiece \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{sectionbox}
\textbf{Your job is to discover the table. Once you know it you can find your way out of the
labyrinth, and until you know it you cannot.}
\end{sectionbox}

That is the win condition and it is epistemic rather than spatial or material. The haul is what you
happened to be carrying when you got out. The openings are the record of what you learned on the
way. Neither is the object. The object is the table, and everything in this note is a way of buying
one row of it.

\subsection{Half of it compresses and half of it does not}

Which is why the game needs two faculties and not one, and it falls out of the table's shape rather
than out of anybody's preference.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.34\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{how big} & \textbf{and so} \\
\midrule
the colour mapping & one rule, a few cases to identify &
\textbf{compressible}. Induce it once and every desire in the labyrinth is known \\[1mm]
interests, motives, secrets & one row per inhabitant, no pattern &
\textbf{incompressible}. There is nothing to induce and each must be got individually \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

So a good player induces the core and buys only as much of the fringe as the way out requires. A
player who tries to enumerate everything runs out of light, and a player who tries to induce
everything discovers there was no rule to find, because interests genuinely are one apiece.

\begin{sectionbox}
And that is the honest reason the note has both an inductive mechanism and a market. Not variety.
The hidden object has a compressible part and an incompressible part, and no single faculty gets at
both.
\end{sectionbox}

\subsection{Which makes the oldest claim in this note literally true}

Section~\ref{sec:updown} argues that the two mappings of a container are not symmetric, that the
way back is where the game lives, and Section~\ref{sec:puzzlepieces} says nothing found at the
bottom is worth anything until it has been carried up.

That has been an argument about architecture with a game draped over it. Under the table it is the
rule.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{5mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.56\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{going down} & costs light and nerve and very little knowledge. Anybody can fall into a
labyrinth \\[1mm]
\textbf{coming up}  & the ways out are operated, in the sense of Section~\ref{sec:living}, by
inhabitants whose prices you must know. So the exit is priced in table rows \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The deeper you went, the more of the table it takes to get back, because there are more operated
ways between you and the surface and each one wants something particular. Which means depth is not
merely risky. Depth is a debt denominated in knowledge, and the descent of
Section~\ref{sec:light} is borrowing against a table you have not read yet.

\begin{alarmbox}
So the failure state has a shape and it is a good one. You are on the sixth level, you have light
for eleven rounds, and the thing operating the way up wants something you cannot identify because
you never found out what its kind wants. Nothing is chasing you. You are simply somewhere you
cannot afford to leave, and you put yourself there one incurious room at a time.
\end{alarmbox}

\subsection{How all of it fits together}
\label{sec:fits}

The note accretes a good many mechanisms and they are not a pile. There are three quantities, three
actions that convert between them, and one of the three quantities is what you win with.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.36\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{measured in} & \textbf{and it is} \\
\midrule
\textbf{light}     & rounds, and descents & the only thing you begin with \\
\textbf{goods}     & weight, out of your capacity & the intermediate, and never the point \\
\textbf{knowledge} & rows of the table & the only thing you need \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=8mm and 20mm]
\node[lvl, minimum width=26mm] (l) {light};
\node[lvl, minimum width=26mm] (g) [right=of l] {goods};
\node[lvl, minimum width=26mm] (k) [right=of g] {knowledge};
\node[leafn, fill=pale] (out) [right=14mm of k] {the way out};
\draw[ar] (l) to[bend left=22] node[lbl, above] {attack} (g);
\draw[ar] (g) to[bend left=22] node[lbl, above] {negotiate} (k);
\draw[ar] (l) to[bend right=30] node[lbl, below] {chat} (k);
\draw[ar] (k) to[loop above] node[lbl, above] {induce} (k);
\draw[ar] (k) -- (out);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

Read the arrows. Attacking turns light and hit points into goods, because a corpse yields what it
carried. Negotiating turns goods into knowledge, at three turns and dagger's length. Chatting turns
light straight into knowledge and skips the middle, which is why the poorest party can still play.
Inducing turns knowledge into more knowledge for nothing, which is the only free move in the game.
And knowledge is what the ways out want.

\begin{sectionbox}
Which explains the three ends of Section~\ref{sec:ends} better than that section does. They are a
balance sheet. \textbf{The haul is goods you did not spend}, so a rich party is a party that failed
to convert. \textbf{The openings are knowledge you did convert.} \textbf{Getting out is whether you
converted enough.} The reason there is no exchange rate between them is that they are three
readings of one conversion, taken at the end.
\end{sectionbox}

\subsection{What each thing is, and what it is for}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.30\textwidth}@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.30\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{what it is} & \textbf{how you get at it} \\
\midrule
a \textbf{kind}      & a flavour of inhabitant. Finitely many, and every one of a kind wants the
same thing &
you see it. It is what a thing visibly is \\[1mm]
a \textbf{desire}    & what a kind wants. An item colour, or a secret &
\emph{induce} it from the rule, or buy it, or ask and be lied to \\[1mm]
a \textbf{secret}    & one true thing an individual knows &
buy it with goods, or be given it for good conversation \\[1mm]
an \textbf{interest} & what one individual likes talking about. Not patterned, one apiece &
buy it from somebody else, or guess from the flavour \\[1mm]
a \textbf{motive}    & what the truth would cost it, which decides what it lies about &
\emph{infer} it from the pattern of what it says. It cannot be asked \\[1mm]
an \textbf{item}     & a colour, a weight, a value and a rarity &
take it from a corpse, a hoard, or a trade \\[1mm]
a \textbf{loadout}   & nought to three weapons, individual &
look at it, before you decide how close to go \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{The two things that refer to each other}

Everything above is a flat list and the game is not flat, because two of the rows point at each
other.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.72\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{A desire may be for a secret. A secret may be what somebody desires.} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

That mutual reference is the whole structure. It makes the inhabitants a graph rather than a
population, it makes a trade a walk on that graph, and it makes the graph's own shape something
worth buying, since a secret may be \emph{what another one wants}.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.32\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{graph} & \textbf{$A \to B$ when} & \textbf{and it is} \\
\midrule
desire   & $A$ wants what $B$ has or knows & the terrain. Walk it backwards to trade \\
interest & $A$ knows what $B$ likes        & the cheap map, paid for in patience \\
knowledge& $A$ knows what $B$ wants        & the dear map, and the most valuable thing there is \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{And where each kind of uncertainty comes from}

Four, and each wants a different faculty, which is why no single kind of cleverness finishes this
game.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.32\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{you do not know} & \textbf{because} & \textbf{so you} \\
\midrule
what a kind wants   & the rule is redrawn each game & \emph{induce}, from cases \\[1mm]
whether you are told the truth & motives are individual & \emph{deduce}, from consistency \\[1mm]
what one likes talking about & it is one apiece and free & \emph{buy}, or guess and waste turns \\[1mm]
whether it is enjoying itself & nothing in the program decides & \emph{be interesting}, and there
is no other route \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{sectionbox}
So the four faculties are induction, deduction, commerce and conversation, and the table of
Section~\ref{sec:table} has a part addressed by each. A player strong at one and hopeless at
another gets a particular distance and no further, which is the breadth of
Section~\ref{sec:breadth} arising from the structure of the hidden object rather than from
balancing.
\end{sectionbox}

\subsection{The populations, and why there are three}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{3mm}}l@{\hspace{3mm}}l@{\hspace{3mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.26\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{armed} & \textbf{moves} & \textbf{and it is for} \\
\midrule
a \textbf{guard}    & yes & no  & being the obstacle, and being the price \\
a \textbf{roamer}   & some & yes & being the market, and the honest evidence of what things take \\
a \textbf{civilian} & no  & no  & being the witness, and the only free information there is \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Each exists because the others cannot do its job. A guard that would chat is a guard you talk past
for free. A civilian that guarded something would make conversation compulsory. A roamer that stood
still would leave nothing in the labyrinth that moved while you were not looking, and
Section~\ref{sec:alive} says that is the difference between a world and a puzzle box.

\begin{sectionbox}
And the last line of the whole arrangement is the one to keep hold of. Light is the only thing you
are given, knowledge is the only thing that gets you out, goods are what the middle of the game is
made of, and there is nothing in the labyrinth that converts knowledge back into light. So every
game is spent one way, and the only question is how much of the table you bought with what you
had.
\end{sectionbox}

\subsection{Puzzle pieces}
\label{sec:puzzlepieces}

Scattered through the lower levels are puzzle pieces. Each one is both a \emph{clue} and an
item. It is a thing you pick up and carry, but it cannot be sold, and having it in your hands
tells you something you did not know. Some lie in rooms and some are carried by monsters, so a
clue can be taken from a corpse, bought in the market, or simply found.

The pieces belong to a riddle, and the riddle is somewhere above where they were found. Guarding
it is a gate-keeper, which is the kind of monster that does not attack and the kind that stands on
a trap door. You give it the pieces you have gathered, and then you try to solve the riddle. This
is a conversation not a command. You put forward your argument and the gate-keeper decides whether
or not to accept it and open the trap door.

\begin{sectionbox}
That is why the way back matters more than the way down. Nothing you find at the bottom is worth
anything until you have carried it back up, and what you have carried back up is the only thing
that makes new depth reachable. In order to fully explore the labyrinth you must unlock it from
below.
\end{sectionbox}

Three things about it are unusual and all three are the point of building it.

The levels are not written by hand. A specification for a level is generated, and the code for
that level is generated from the specification, and the level is then a program like any other.
So the labyrinth is different every time and nobody has to design it.

The monsters are non-deterministic. Each one is a language model with a finite set of actions,
which you can speak to in plain English. You can threaten them, lie to them,
offer them money or ask them what is down the passage, and what they do about this is their own
business.

The puzzles are non-deterministic too, and nothing in the game knows their answers. A puzzle is
solved when the monster keeping it is persuaded, and being persuaded is the only sense in which
it is solved.

\subsection{The party}
\label{sec:party}

A party is the group of people you take down, and for the purposes of this decomposition it is
one thing rather than several. It has a number of hands, a pool of hit points shared between
them, a stock of experience gained by getting past things, and whatever it is carrying. It acts
once in a round, because the word you type is given to the party and not to a person inside it.
When the note says the party is wounded, it means the pool is low and not that a named
individual is hurt.

That is a decision and not an oversight. A party of three who each act separately would be a
tensor, standing opposite the tensor of monsters facing them, and it would be the better game.
It would also make a round $(a \boxtimes b \boxtimes c) \lhd \text{monsters}$, and every rule
about actions would have to be written per person rather than per party. This note describes the
smaller version, and the larger one is the obvious first thing to add.

\subsection{The roster}
\label{sec:roster}

A party is not the same thing as everybody you have, and the difference is where the game keeps
its longest-range decision.

At the surface there is a roster of people. A party is a subset of the roster that you send down,
and how many go is chosen before each descent. People who die do not come back, and the roster
does not refill.

So a person is two things at once, and this is the point of having a roster at all. A person is a
hand, which is carrying capacity on this descent. A person is also a body, which is a descent you
can still make later. Spending one to bring up a relic is spending the other, and the two costs
fall due at different times.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{6mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.60\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{the roster} & everybody left alive at the surface. Never grows. \\
\textbf{the party}  & who is going down this time, and therefore how many hands there are \\
\textbf{the muster} & the choice of how many to send, made before each descent \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{sectionbox}
Send four and you carry more and lose more when it goes wrong. Send two and you carry little and
keep a reserve for a trip you have not planned yet. That is the muster, it is a product, and it is
the only decision in the game whose consequences are two descents away.
\end{sectionbox}

A party can also be lost outright, and what happens then is settled by a decision already taken
elsewhere. Section~\ref{sec:kept} keeps every level alive after the party has climbed out of it,
so a loss takes the people and leaves the labyrinth exactly where it stood. Every lock opened from
below is still open, every level already written is still written, and the map of
Section~\ref{sec:map} is still drawn. The next party is drawn from whoever is left.

\begin{sectionbox}
Which is the property of Section~\ref{sec:multi} used serially by one player instead of
concurrently by two. People are what you lose and the labyrinth is what you keep. Nothing new has
to be built for it, because the levels were being kept anyway.
\end{sectionbox}

\subsection{Multi-party}
\label{sec:multi}

The levels are servers and a server may have many clients, so two parties descending at once is
not a new capability. It is a capability the architecture already has but is not yet using.
Nothing about the two mappings changes: each party is its own descent, its own tree, and its own way back, because
a response goes to whoever asked for it.

What it buys is worth more than it costs. If a party unlocks a door from below, that door is open
when the next party arrives, so the labyrinth is unlocked between them rather than by each of
them separately. Each party can gather separate pieces.

The cost.

A level has to hold state, and once it does, the same prompt no longer gives the same response,
because somebody else has been through. That is the same concession already made for the
monsters rather than a new one. Apparently Neil has a solution for this using \emph{workers}.

If two parties are in the same room at the same time, who goes first? Nothing said so far decides
that. A combinator says how prompts and responses fit together, but it has nothing to say about
whose turn it is.

So a rule has to be chosen, and then an operator will express it. The tidy choice is that the two
parties are a tensor, exactly as the monsters in the room are. Both are prompted with the state
as it stood at the start of the round, both owe a result, and neither of them sees what the other
did until the round is over. Nobody goes first, because they all go at once.

Potentially two parties could reach for the same thing at the same time. This is the ordinary
problem of shared state, but the architecture has solved it already, because each level is one
program with one flow of control. Requests to a level are served one at a time, so the state moves from one
consistent position to the next without anything having to be locked. That is the reason the
whole game is built out of servers rather than function calls in the first place.

\newpage

\section{Containers}
\label{sec:containers}

The decomposition that follows is built out of one idea, and this section is all of it. Nothing
here is particular to dungeons.

\subsection{An interface is a pair}

A \emph{container} is an interface, written as two things together. The first is the set of
prompts you may send. The second gives, for each prompt, the type of the response.

The second half is what makes it more than a list. The response's type depends on which prompt was
sent, so a single interface can respond with a number to one question and a list of rooms to
another, and which you get is fixed by what you asked. Write the pair as $(P, R)$, where $P$ is
the prompts and $R\,p$ is the type of response to the prompt $p$.

One condition runs through everything below. The set of prompts must be \emph{finite}. Over a
finite set, a response type that varies with the prompt is just a table, and a table is something an
ordinary type system can write down. Over an infinite set it is not, and none of this works.

\subsection{A morphism is two mappings}

Interfaces are joined by \emph{morphisms}, and a morphism is a pair of mappings going in opposite
directions. Given interfaces $(P, R)$ above and $(Q, T)$ below,

\[ u : P \to Q \qquad\qquad f : (p : P) \to T\,(u\,p) \to R\,p. \]

The mapping $u$ takes a prompt you were given and computes the prompt to send below. The mapping
$f$ takes the response that came back and turns it into the response you owe. Control flows down
through $u$. Data flows back up through $f$.

Read the type of $f$ slowly, because the whole of this note leans on it. It is given the original
prompt \emph{as well as} the response from below, and what it must produce is a response to the original
prompt. So the way back up has access to the way down, and the two are not symmetric.

\subsection{A command-line tool is already a container}

None of this requires a special language. A command-line tool receives one prompt, its argument
vector, and produces a response whose type depends on which prompt it was. Ask one thing and you get
a list of commits. Ask another and you get a working tree. Those are not the same kind of thing,
and which you get is fixed by the word you typed.

That is a container, and it is why the levels in this game are separate programs. One level
reaches the one below it by spawning a command-line tool that carries a prompt to a socket, so
the delegation is a real process with a real boundary rather than a function call wearing a
costume.

\subsection{Nothing checks what crosses}
\label{sec:crosses}

The boundary is text. A prompt goes out as an argument vector and a response comes back on standard
output, and no type survives the crossing. What arrives is bytes.

So every claim made above is established, if it is established at all, by a \emph{decoder} on the
receiving side: a function which takes what arrived and either produces a value of the type that
was promised or fails. The type says what must be true. The decoder is the only thing that ever
checks it. That is the arrangement throughout this game, and Section~\ref{sec:agents} is where it
matters most, because there the thing on the other side of the boundary is a language model.

\subsection{What is a container here, and what is not}
\label{sec:roles}

Before the expression, a distinction the note has been relying on without stating, and its absence
is the reason the algebra gets confusing once rooms have squares in them.

A container is a set of prompts together with, for each prompt, the type of the response. So the
test for whether something belongs in the algebra is one question, and it is not a question about
how important the thing is.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.72\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Is it prompted, and does something come back?} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Ask it of everything in the game and the answers sort into three groups, not two.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.34\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{role} & \textbf{and therefore} \\
\midrule
a level    & \textbf{container} & prompted with a party, answers with a descent \\
a room     & \textbf{container} & prompted with a turn, answers with what happened \\
a monster  & \textbf{container} & prompted with words, answers with one of eight actions \\
a gate-keeper   & \textbf{container} & prompted with an argument, answers with a verdict \\
the market & \textbf{container} & prompted with a purchase, answers with what you now hold \\
\midrule
a square   & \emph{an index}    & appears \emph{inside} a prompt. \texttt{Move to (3,2)} \\
an item    & \emph{an index}    & appears inside a prompt. \texttt{Offer the green stone} \\
a weapon   & \emph{an index}    & likewise, and so does a colour \\
\midrule
the grid's contents & \emph{state} & remembered by a room, never crosses a boundary \\
which guards are dead & \emph{state} & likewise \\
which ways stand open & \emph{state} & likewise \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{The grid is an index, not an operator}

Which is the specific confusion worth heading off, because there is an inviting wrong answer.

A room is a rectangle of squares, squares seem independent, and independence is what
$\boxtimes$ is for. So a room looks like a tensor of squares. It is not, and it fails the test twice
over.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{5mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.58\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{nothing prompts a square} & There is no question you put to the square at $(3,2)$ and no
answer it owes you. It has no interface, so it cannot be a fibre of anything \\[1mm]
\textbf{squares are not independent} & Movement couples them. You may reach $(3,2)$ only from a
square beside it, and a tensor asserts that neither side can see the other \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{sectionbox}
So the algebra composes the things that \emph{answer}, and geometry is what one of them
\emph{remembers}. That is the whole of the distinction. A room is one container holding a grid, not
a composite of many containers arranged in a rectangle.
\end{sectionbox}

\subsection{But squares are in the type system, one level down}

They are not absent from the types. They are in a different position in them, and seeing where is
what makes the whole thing click.

Section~\ref{sec:containers} says a container is a pair of a shape type and, for each shape, a
position type, and that the shape type is a \emph{finite index}, being a tagged union. The squares
of a room are finite. So they can index a shape.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{the prompt} & \textbf{its shape} & \textbf{the response} \\
\midrule
walk one square   & \texttt{\{tag:"Move", to: Square\}}      & where you now stand \\
strike            & \texttt{\{tag:"Strike", with: Weapon\}}  & what it took off, or a miss \\
offer             & \texttt{\{tag:"Offer", give: Item[]\}}   & a secret, or nothing \\
say something     & \texttt{\{tag:"Say", words: string\}}    & one of eight actions \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Read the middle column. \texttt{Square}, \texttt{Weapon} and \texttt{Item} all appear there, inside
a shape, as the index of a prompt. That is what it means for the geometry to be typed without being
composed.

\begin{sectionbox}
And it is the same trick as the loot table of Section~\ref{sec:types}. A relic on level one is
unwriteable because the item type is indexed by depth. A move to a square outside the room is
unwriteable because the move's shape is indexed by that room's squares. Neither needed an operator.
Both needed an index.
\end{sectionbox}

\subsection{So the third column is where the danger is}

The state row is the one to keep honest, because state is what the algebra does not see and
therefore what the algebra cannot promise anything about.

A room holding a grid, a set of corpses and a record of which ways stand open is a room whose
answers depend on things no prompt mentioned. Section~\ref{sec:multi} already conceded that when it
allowed two parties, and Section~\ref{sec:alive} leans on it hard when it has the market settle
between visits.

\begin{alarmbox}
Which is worth writing down as a rule rather than discovering later. Everything in the state column
is invisible to the type system and to every combinator in this note. So the only guarantees about
it are the ones something checks explicitly, which is why the generator conditions of
Section~\ref{sec:graph} are re-checked after every settle and why redundant discharge exists. The
algebra protects the boundaries. It does not protect the memory behind them.
\end{alarmbox}

\subsection{Down is not the same as up}
\label{sec:updown}

The party that arrives at level four is not the party that entered level three. Gold has been
found, wounds have been taken and lamp oil has been extinguished. That is the mapping $u$.

The mapping $u$ is given one thing and the mapping $f$ is given two. Its type is
$f : (p : P) \to T\,(u\,p) \to R\,p$, so it receives the prompt that went down as well as the
response that came back.

Suppose the party finds a key on level four.
There is a locked door on level three, and the party walked past it on the way in because they
had nothing to open it with. On the way out the mapping $f$ of level three is handed the response
from below, the key is in it, and the door is now a door the party can open.

A puzzle is the same mechanism and it is what the game is built on. A gate-keeper on level two wants
three pieces, and the pieces are on levels four, five and six. The party cannot satisfy it on the
way in and does not need to. The pieces come up through the responses, and the mapping $f$ of level
two is handed them along with everything else, so the argument can be made on the way out and
not before. What the labyrinth is really made of is not the way down. It is what the way down
lets you do on the way back.

\section{The tower}

Eight levels, eight ports, and the same program running on each of them.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=6mm]
\node[font=\small\itshape] (you) {you};
\node[lvl, below=6mm of you] (l1) {level 1 :9001};
\node[lvl, below=of l1] (l2) {level 2 :9002};
\node[lvl, below=of l2] (l3) {level 3 :9003};
\node[font=\small, below=4mm of l3] (dots) {$\vdots$};
\node[lvl, below=4mm of dots] (l8) {level 8 :9008};

\foreach \a/\b in {you/l1, l1/l2, l2/l3}
  \draw[ar] ($(\a.south)+(-8mm,0)$) -- ($(\b.north)+(-8mm,0)$);
\draw[ar] ($(l3.south)+(-8mm,0)$) -- ($(dots.north)+(-8mm,0)$);
\draw[ar] ($(dots.south)+(-8mm,0)$) -- ($(l8.north)+(-8mm,0)$);

\foreach \a/\b in {l1/you, l2/l1, l3/l2}
  \draw[ar] ($(\a.north)+(8mm,0)$) -- ($(\b.south)+(8mm,0)$);
\draw[ar] ($(dots.north)+(8mm,0)$) -- ($(l3.south)+(8mm,0)$);
\draw[ar] ($(l8.north)+(8mm,0)$) -- ($(dots.south)+(8mm,0)$);

\node[lbl, left=1mm of l2, xshift=-14mm, align=right] {the party\\descends\\through $u$};
\node[lbl, right=1mm of l2, xshift=14mm, align=left] {the haul\\returns\\through $f$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

Eight bespoke programs would be a game. The same program composed with itself eight times is a
claim about composition, and the claim is the reason for building it.

The eight are not eight copies of the same content. Each is started with a level specification
and serves whatever that specification describes, so the program is the same and the labyrinth
is not.

\subsection{It could be a tree and not a chain}

The picture above is the simplest case and the equations are already more general than it. Every
level ends in a choice of ways down, and each way leads to a level which ends in a choice of its
own, so what the recursion describes is a tree. Eight in a line is what you get by never
branching.

The depth of that tree is fixed before the game starts, and it is finite. That is not a
concession, it is the condition everything else rests on. A finite depth is what lets a rule
about what may be found at a given depth be a table, and a table is what the type system can
hold. A labyrinth that went down for ever would have no such table, and nothing in
Section~\ref{sec:types} would work.

A tree is a maze with no way of walking in a circle, and that is what this labyrinth is. The
generator may make it as large and as tangled as it likes. What it may not do is join a passage
back onto one the party has already come down, because the way out is the way in reversed and
there is nothing to reverse in a circle.

\section{The decomposition}
\label{sec:decomp}

Interfaces compose, in four different ways, and all four appear below. A fifth operator appears
too, written $\seqb$. It is the sequential one again, with a second prompt that may be chosen
after the first response arrives, and Section~\ref{sec:agents} explains why that has to be written
by hand rather than taken ready-made.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}c@{\hspace{5mm}}l@{\hspace{5mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.52\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{reads as} & \textbf{what it means here} \\
\midrule
$\boxtimes$   & both, and both respond    & two parts of the game that do not touch each other \\
$\times$      & both offered, one responds & two uses of a thing you have only one of \\
$+$           & one side, chosen first    & the prompt already belongs to one side \\
$\lhd$        & then                      & the second prompt is computed from the first response \\
$\seqb$       & then, with a choice       & the first response decides which side is prompted \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{The whole game, as an expression}

\begin{align*}
\text{game}       \;&=\; \text{descent} \;\lhd\; \text{game} \quad\text{until the descents run out} \\[1.5mm]
\text{game}(0)    \;&=\; \text{reckoning} \\[1.5mm]
\text{reckoning}  \;&=\; \text{haul} \;\boxtimes\; \text{opened} \;\boxtimes\; \text{bottom} \\[3mm]
\text{descent}    \;&=\; \text{muster} \;\lhd\; \text{outfit} \;\lhd\; \text{level}(1) \;\lhd\; \text{market} \\[1.5mm]
\text{muster}     \;&=\; \text{one} \;\times\; \text{two} \;\times\; \cdots \;\times\; \text{r} \\[1.5mm]
\text{outfit}     \;&=\; \text{smith} \;\times\; \text{chandler} \\[1.5mm]
\text{market}     \;&=\; \text{sell} \;\lhd\; \text{buy} \\[3mm]
\text{room}(d)    \;&=\; \text{round} \;\lhd\; \text{room}(d) \;\lhd\; \text{exit}(d) \\[1.5mm]
\text{exit}(d)    \;&=\; e_1 \;\times\; e_2 \;\times\; \cdots \;\times\; e_k \quad\text{the doorways, and you take one} \\[1.5mm]
e_i               \;&=\; \text{room}(d) \;+\; \text{room}(d{+}1) \quad\text{laterally, or down, and possibly one you have seen} \\[1.5mm]
\text{room}(9)    \;&=\; \text{nothing. There is no ninth level} \\[3mm]
\text{round}      \;&=\; \text{move} \;\times\; \text{talk} \;\times\; \text{stab} \;\times\; \text{trade} \;\times\; \text{use} \\[1.5mm]
\text{talk}       \;&=\; \text{chat} \;+\; \text{ask} \;+\; \text{offer} \quad\text{all of them on its square} \\[1.5mm]
\text{liking}     \;&=\; \text{liking} \;\lhd\; \text{talk} \quad\text{it moves with every word} \\[3mm]
\text{chat}       \;&=\; \text{say} \;\seqb\; \bigl( \text{told} \;+\; \text{nothing} \;+\; \text{attacked} \bigr) \\[1.5mm]
\text{offer}      \;&=\; \text{give} \;\seqb\; \bigl( \text{honoured} \;+\; \text{robbed} \bigr) \\[1.5mm]
\text{puzzle}(d)  \;&=\; \text{gate-keeper}(d) \;\seqb\; \bigl( \text{wing}(d) \;+\; \text{refused} \bigr)
\end{align*}

Every line is one rule of the game, and the next section states which.

Three of those lines are worth reading twice, and Section~\ref{sec:defn} unpacks the first of
them.

There is no separate $\text{rooms}(d)$ and no separate $\text{way}(d)$, because a room \emph{is} a
way and whoever stands on it is the price of taking it. The product over rooms and the sum over
ways down were the same decision written twice, which is the whole of
Section~\ref{sec:oneguard}.

The line for $\text{liking}$ is the only place in the note where a $\lhd$ carries something that is
neither a party nor a response, and Section~\ref{sec:liking} is about why it is the centre of the
game. It accumulates over turns, so every word is prompted with what the last word did.

And $\text{chat}$ has three branches where every other composite has two. Told, nothing, or
attacked, and which of the three arrives is the inhabitant's decision after you have spoken. That
is the $\seqb$ doing the most work it does anywhere here.

\subsection{One rule for each operator}

Split in two, because the operators divide by how often the player meets them. The first table is
the shape of a whole game and the second is the shape of a round.

\small
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{5mm}}c@{\hspace{5mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.48\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{part} & & \textbf{the rule that forces it} \\
\midrule
\textbf{the game} & $\lhd$     & \textbf{each descent is prompted with what the last one left behind}, and the count of descents is what makes the chain finite. Nothing refills it. \\
\textbf{the reckoning}& $\boxtimes$ & \textbf{the three ends are computed independently and nothing joins them.} Each is answered from the same final state, none is a function of another, and no operator anywhere adds them together. A tensor is exactly the refusal to give a score. \\
the muster        & $\times$   & the roster offers every party size it can afford and exactly one of them goes. A person is a hand on this trip and a descent later, so this is the one choice whose cost falls due twice. \\
outfitting        & $\times$   & you have one purse, so a sword, a bow and a torch compete for it. Light bought is depth bought, which makes the chandler the least glamorous and most often correct answer. \\
the market        & $\lhd$     & what you can afford to buy is computed from what you sold. \\
\textbf{the rooms}& $\times$   & \textbf{each room is one way onward with one guard on it, and getting past any of them descends.} Three rooms is three prices and you have one purse, so the other two are an offer and not a duty. A room you walk away from keeps its guard and its way. \\
a guarded way     & $+$        & dealing is answered by the monster and retreating is answered by the map, so which branch is prompted is settled by which of the two you chose. \\
your round        & $\times$   & move, talk, stab, trade or use, and exactly one of them, because a round is one action and all five spend the same light. \\
\textbf{what you say}& $\seqb$    & \textbf{you speak and the thing decides what that was worth.} Told, nothing, or attacked. Not knowable when you open your mouth, which is what makes conversation the sequential operator and not a menu. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{5mm}}c@{\hspace{5mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.48\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{part} & & \textbf{the rule that forces it} \\
\midrule
\textbf{a command}& $+$        & \textbf{the options depend on where you are standing, and which of them you type decides who responds.} A blow goes to the monsters, a price goes to the market, a direction goes to the map. The three respond with different kinds of thing. \\
a round           & $\lhd$     & the monsters respond to your attack. Your blow is thrown first and the monsters respond to the result. \\
liking            & $\lhd$     & what you say next is prompted with where the last thing you said left it. This is the only accumulating quantity in the game. \\
the monsters      & $\boxtimes$ & every monster in the room acts at the same time. \\
the guard's answer& $\seqb$    & you make the offer and the monster decides whether that settled it, so the branch is not knowable when the goods leave your hands. \\
\textbf{a bargain}& $\seqb$    & \textbf{you hand the goods over and the monster decides whether that settled it.} Nothing enforces a bargain underground, so the second branch is being robbed, and that is why fighting stays worth doing at an affordable price. \\
\textbf{a puzzle} & $\seqb$    & \textbf{the gate-keeper is asked first, and the gate-keeper's verdict decides what is prompted next.} Persuade it and a wing of the labyrinth is entered. Fail and nothing happens. The verdict is not knowable when the argument is made, which is what makes this the sequential operator and not a choice made in advance. \\
what you carry    & $\times$   & you have a fixed number of hands. Loot, keys, pieces, torches and arrows all want one, so what you bring up is chosen against what you leave behind. \\
the descent       & $\lhd$     & what waits on level $d{+}1$ is computed from what you did on level $d$, and the largest part of what is computed is how much light you have left. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\normalsize

\subsection{The command is the routing}
\label{sec:routing}

The player types one word. Where the party is standing decides who is allowed to respond to it, and
the three who might respond do not respond with the same kind of thing.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{6mm}}l@{\hspace{6mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.40\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{the word} & \textbf{who responds} & \textbf{what comes back} \\
\midrule
\texttt{strike} & the monsters in the room & who was hurt, and by how much \\
\texttt{buy}    & the market on the surface & what you now carry, and what it cost \\
\texttt{north}  & the map of this level     & where the party is now standing \\
\texttt{say}    & whatever is listening      & what it decided to do about that \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Three interfaces, genuinely different, and the word names which one the prompt belongs to before
it is sent. That is a sum, and unlike the trap door it is a sum on every turn of the game rather
than once a level.

The set of words that exist is itself a function of where you are. In the market there is no
monster to strike, and in a room with a monster in it there is nothing to buy.

\subsection{Fighting}
\label{sec:fighting}

A round has three parts and each is a different operator.

You act first, and you have one action. Swing the weapon in your hand, spend an arrow, or put
the weapon away and draw the other one. All three are offered and exactly one of them responds,
because a round is one action and drawing is an action like any other. That is the product.

Every monster in the room is somewhere between one step away and five. A sword reaches a monster
at one step. A bow reaches one at three steps or more, and every shot spends an arrow out of a
quiver that holds few. At two steps neither weapon reaches anything, which is a gap you get out
of by drawing or by backing away.

An arrow that has been fired is lying in the room, one step from whatever it hit. So the quiver
is refilled by walking into sword range of the thing you were trying not to be near, and the
bow is a weapon that pays for its own distance.

A monster moves one step in a round if it decides to. Approaching and withdrawing are two of the
things it can do, alongside striking and talking, so where it is standing is something it decides
rather than something the room fixes.

\begin{sectionbox}
Which turns distance into a count of rounds. A monster five steps off gives you three shots
before it arrives, and every round you spend putting the bow away is one it spends closing.
\end{sectionbox}

Then the monsters respond. All of them act, all of them owe a result, and none of them waits for
another to finish, so the monsters in a room are a tensor. A room with three monsters is
$m_1 \boxtimes m_2 \boxtimes m_3$, and the independence is enforced rather than promised. All
three prompts have to be built before any is sent, so the third monster's move cannot be computed from
what the first one did.

And the round as a whole is sequential. What the monsters do is computed from what you did, which
is the entire reason a round is a round.

That is where the distance earns its keep. You spend a round drawing the bow while the room is
still far away, they spend it closing, and you finish the round holding a weapon that no longer
reaches anything. The choice was made before their answer and it lives or dies by it, which is
what the sequential operator is for.

\subsection{One level, drawn}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=7mm and 12mm]
\node[leafn, fill=pale] (lev) {level($d$)};
\node[op, draw=seq, text=seq] (s1) [right=10mm of lev] {$\lhd$};
\draw[ar] (lev) -- (s1);

\node[op, draw=prod, text=prod] (t1) [above right=17mm and 12mm of s1] {$\times$};
\node[op, draw=sumc, text=sumc] (p1) [below right=17mm and 12mm of s1] {$+$};
\draw[ar] (s1) -- node[lbl, above, sloped] {rooms} (t1);
\draw[ar] (s1) -- node[lbl, below, sloped] {way down} (p1);

\node[leafn] (east)  [above right=7mm and 14mm of t1] {east};
\node[leafn] (west)  [right=14mm of t1]               {west};
\node[leafn] (north) [below right=7mm and 14mm of t1] {north};
\draw[ar] (t1) -- (east);
\draw[ar] (t1) -- (west);
\draw[ar] (t1) -- (north);

\node[leafn] (stair) [above right=7mm and 14mm of p1] {mines($d{+}1$)};
\node[leafn] (shaft) [below right=7mm and 14mm of p1] {caverns($d{+}1$)};
\draw[ar] (p1) -- node[lbl, above, sloped] {trap door} (stair);
\draw[ar] (p1) -- node[lbl, below, sloped] {shaft} (shaft);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

Read it left to right. A room is cleared, and only then is the way down chosen. Each leaf at
the bottom right is another whole level, which is where the recursion is. The three rooms are a
product and not a tensor, so the trap door waits on one of them and the other two are an
invitation you may decline.

\subsection{Where the recursion is}
\label{sec:recursion}

Only one equation mentions itself.

\[ \text{level}(d) \;=\; \bigl( \text{east}(d) \times \text{west}(d) \times \text{north}(d) \bigr) \;\lhd\; \bigl( \text{mines}(d{+}1) + \text{caverns}(d{+}1) \bigr) \]

Expand it eight times and the dungeon is a right-nested chain of sequential compositions with a
tensor hanging off each link. That is the whole architecture in one line, and it is also why the
depth of the delegation and the depth of the dungeon are the same number.

Expanded once it is a chain. Expanded at both ways down it is a tree, and the tree is the
labyrinth. The expansion stops at the depth the game was given, which is why the recursion
terminates and why the tables of Section~\ref{sec:types} exist. What gets built is the path the
party actually took, one level at a time, so a generated child need not exist until somebody
goes that way.

\newpage

\newpage

\section{The room is a grid}
\label{sec:grid}

\begin{center}
\begin{tcolorbox}[plainbg, colback=pale, width=0.86\textwidth]
\begin{align*}
\text{level}(d) \;&=\; \text{east}(d) \;\times\; \text{west}(d) \;\times\; \text{north}(d) \\
\text{east}(d)  \;&=\; \text{room} \;\lhd\; \text{level}(d{+}1) \\
\text{room}     \;&=\; \text{round} \;\lhd\; \text{room}
\end{align*}
\end{tcolorbox}
\end{center}

Three lines, and this section is what the squares underneath them are for. The product is which
room, the chain is what the room leads to, and the inner chain is turns going by.


Distance has been a number between one and five and that was doing too little work. So a room is a
rectangle of squares, everything in it stands on one, and a turn moves you one square.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.52]
\draw[step=1, color=gray!45] (0,0) grid (9,5);
\node at (0.5,2.5) {\small\bfseries P};
\node at (5.5,2.5) {\small M};
\node at (8.5,1.5) {\small$\ast$};
\node[font=\scriptsize\itshape, color=grey] at (2.5,-0.55) {five squares, three turns of closing};
\draw[ar, ->] (1.1,2.5) -- (2.4,2.5);
\draw[ar, ->] (4.9,2.5) -- (3.6,2.5);
\node[font=\scriptsize\itshape, color=grey] at (8.5,0.4) {the way};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

The geometry is the point and not the presentation. Three weapons, three bands of distance, and
they do not overlap.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{\hspace{4mm}}l@{\hspace{4mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.30\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{weapon} & \textbf{reaches} & \textbf{strength} & \textbf{and elsewhere} \\
\midrule
a dagger     & the same square, which is dagger's length & least & useless at any distance at all \\
a long sword & one square, adjacent   & most  & too long to bring to bear on your own square, too short at two \\
a bow        & two squares or more    & middling & cannot be drawn inside two \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{sectionbox}
So every distance has exactly one weapon that works at it, and at any moment two of your three are
dead weight. The product of Section~\ref{sec:decomp} stops being a table with eight misses in it
and becomes a partition. Where you are standing decides what you are holding, and changing your
mind about that costs a turn.
\end{sectionbox}

Strength is the second axis and it does not agree with range. The long sword is the strongest thing
in the game and it works at exactly one square, so the strongest blow available is available at the
distance you are hardest pressed to hold. The dagger is the weakest and it works where nothing else
does. And because a weapon has a weight as well, the same non-domination condition applies here as
Section~\ref{sec:items} imposes on loot.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.72\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{No weapon may beat another on range, strength and weight at once.} Each must be the answer
to something. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{One way, one guard, three answers}
\label{sec:oneguard}

The simplification that made the rest of this fit.

A room holds one monster and one thing it is standing on. What it is standing on is either a way
onward or an item worth carrying. Nothing else is in the room worth deciding about, so the party
has exactly three answers available, and every level is that question asked as many times as it
has rooms.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{5mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.58\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{a guarded way}  & getting past it descends. Some room on every level must be one of
these or the level is a dead end. \\
\textbf{a guarded hoard}& getting past it yields the item. Optional in every sense, and it is
where the haul comes from. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

That distinction does more work than its size suggests, and it is the reason to state it. A level
with one way down and two hoards puts the first end against the third in every room the party
walks into. Take the hoards and you are richer and one descent nearer the end of the game. Walk to
the way down and you are deeper and carrying nothing. The muster of Section~\ref{sec:roster} asked
that question once a trip and now the level asks it three times a level.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{5mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.58\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{attack}   & pays in the physical economy if it works, and in people if it does not. What
it was standing on is now yours, whether that was a way or an item \\
\textbf{negotiate}& pays in the information economy if it works, and it can only be attempted from
one square away, which Section~\ref{sec:grid} is about \\
\textbf{retreat}  & is walking, and it leaves the guard where it was \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\[ \text{round} \;=\; \text{move} \;\times\; \text{strike} \;\times\; \text{chat} \;\times\; \text{negotiate} \;\times\; \text{trade} \;\times\; \text{draw} \;\times\; \text{use} \]

One product, seven branches, one action a turn. Retreating is not a separate operator because it is
walking, which Section~\ref{sec:grid} makes literal.

\subsection{What it buys, and it is more than tidiness}

\textbf{Two operators collapse into one.} The old note had a product over rooms and then a sum over
ways down, and treated them as separate decisions. They never were. Clearing a room was only ever
worth doing because of where the room led, so choosing a room and choosing a way were the same
choice counted twice.

\textbf{The room product becomes a choice of price.} Three rooms is three guards with three
desires, and one purse to meet them with. So the question at a level is not which room is easiest.
It is which of these three prices can I pay, and the answer depends on what the party is carrying
and what it has learned.

\textbf{And it becomes a choice of end.} Because a room is either a way or a hoard, the same
product asks whether this level was for getting deeper or for getting richer. Three ends and no
exchange rate between them, met as a concrete question, three times a level, in front of a monster
whose price you may not know.

\begin{sectionbox}
Which puts \texttt{desireVisibility} at the centre of the game rather than at the edge of one
monster. A party that knows all three desires is shopping. A party that knows none is opening
conversations at a round apiece to find out, and the light does not care that the information was
worth having.
\end{sectionbox}

\textbf{Retreat becomes a real option with a real price.} It was previously the \texttt{move} branch
of a turn and did nothing in particular. Now it is how you decline a price, it costs a round, and
retreating from all three rooms of a level has spent three rounds and opened nothing at all.

\subsection{And what it costs}

One thing is lost and it should be said rather than quietly dropped.

The monsters of a room were a tensor, $m_1 \boxtimes m_2 \boxtimes \cdots \boxtimes m_k$, and the
note used them as its clearest example of independence being enforced rather than promised, since
every prompt is built before any is sent. With one guard to a way, $k$ is one and the tensor
degenerates.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\hspace{5mm}}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.58\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{where $\boxtimes$ still lives} & the reckoning of Section~\ref{sec:ends}, which computes
three ends from one state and joins none of them. Two parties descending at once, in
Section~\ref{sec:multi}. And the ladder of Section~\ref{sec:depth}, which is four policies run on
one setting where none may see another. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

So the operator keeps three homes and loses its most vivid one. That is a fair trade for a level
whose rules fit in three lines, and the doubly guarded way is still expressible if it is ever
wanted. It is $k = 2$, the operator is already written, and nothing else in the note would have to
change.

\subsection{You can only negotiate within dagger range}

This is the rule that reorganises everything, and it is worth stating on its own line.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.72\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{A monster will not talk to you from across a room. To negotiate you must be on its square,
which is dagger's length, and dagger's length is the closest anything gets to anything.} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Diplomacy is therefore not the safe route. It is the route that requires you to close all the way,
and closing takes turns during which the thing is also moving, and it ends with you standing on the
square occupied by whatever it was you were trying to form an opinion about.

\begin{sectionbox}
